People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Like I’m a disappointment, and... maybe everyone would be better off without me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about being a danger to people and a disappointment?\n\nPatient: About a year... maybe a little over. It started after I finished high school and began taking college classes while working full-time at the clinic. So... about a year and two months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like, or has that changed over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah... it’s changed. I used to jog, or knit, cook, read after class—stuff like that. Now I mostly just end up sitting on my bed in my scrubs and... I can’t really make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in the things you do?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean... I used to feel better after jogging, or I’d knit or cook, read after class. Now I mostly just sit on my bed still in my scrubs and... I don’t really care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts of harming yourself?\n\nPatient: I… not like a plan or anything. I haven’t… decided to hurt myself. But I do think sometimes it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up, or if I could disappear so I wouldn’t mess things up again.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, those things used to help—jogging, knitting, cooking, reading after class. Now I just kind of sit on my bed after work, still in my scrubs, and I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still give you some pleasure?\n\nPatient: Honestly… no. Not much. Even small stuff like reading for a bit or cooking something simple just feels like too much, and I end up sitting on my bed in my scrubs, not really caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to jog or knit, or cook something, read after class… it helped clear my head. Now I mostly just sit on my bed in my scrubs and I can’t really make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I think. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but my work pants are getting loose. I kind of forget to eat until late, and then it’s just… crackers or coffee sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’m slower. People at the clinic have asked if I’m tired, because I take longer getting supplies and sometimes I just… need a few seconds to answer even simple questions.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before all this, I guess. It’s been like this for about a year or so—maybe a year and two months. It started after I finished high school and started college classes and working full-time at the clinic. Before that I felt more like myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I used to jog or knit, or cook something, read after class… stuff like that. Most days I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling, and I’m kind of just… holding it together.\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness and hopelessness present for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s there most of the day, I think. I wake up with that heavy, empty feeling, and even if I’m smiling at patients or trying to act normal at the clinic… it doesn’t really leave. I’m just kind of holding it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. More like a little over a year, maybe a year and two months. It started after high school, when I began college classes and working full-time at the clinic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where you felt significantly better than you do most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can seem better sometimes, like at the clinic when I’m smiling with patients or speaking Spanish with them, but inside it’s still that heavy, empty feeling. Now I just kind of sit on my bed after work, still in my scrubs, and I can’t make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Are there any small things that still give you some pleasure?\n\nPatient: Honestly… no. Not much. Even small stuff like reading for a bit or cooking something simple just feels like too much, and I end up sitting on my bed in my scrubs, not really caring.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I used to jog or knit, or cook something, read after class… it helped clear my head. Now I mostly just sit on my bed in my scrubs and I can’t really make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a little, I think. I haven’t been trying to lose weight, but my work pants are getting loose. I kind of forget to eat until late, and then it’s just… crackers or coffee sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to or having trouble sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I’m slower. People at the clinic have asked if I’m tired, because I take longer getting supplies and sometimes I just… need a few seconds to answer even simple questions.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in the past where you felt significantly better than you do now?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before all this, I guess. It’s been like this for about a year or so—maybe a year and two months. It started after I finished high school and started college classes and working full-time at the clinic. Before that I felt more like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt little to no pleasure in doing things for most of the past year?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time. I used to jog or knit, or cook something, read after class… stuff like that. Now I mostly just sit on my bed in my scrubs and I can’t really make myself care about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods over the past year where your feelings of sadness and hopelessness went away for a little while?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, maybe I can look okay for a bit at work, smiling with patients and speaking Spanish with them, but it doesn’t really go away. Most days I wake up with this heavy, empty feeling, and I’m kind of just… holding it together.\n\nDoctor: Are your feelings of sadness and hopelessness present for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s there most of the day, I think. I wake up with that heavy, empty feeling, and even if I’m smiling at patients or trying to act normal at the clinic… it doesn’t really leave. I’m just kind of holding it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this way for more than two years?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. More like a little over a year, maybe a year and two months. It started after high school, when I began college classes and working full-time at the clinic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where you felt significantly better than you do most of the time?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I can seem better sometimes, like at the clinic when I’m smiling with patients or speaking Spanish with them, but inside it’s still that heavy, empty feeling. I’m mostly just trying to keep it together.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days recently where you felt happy or okay, even if it was just for a part of the day?\n\nPatient: Maybe for moments, but not really a whole day. Most days I wake up with that heavy, empty feeling, and even if I’m smiling with patients or trying to act normal at the clinic, it’s still there. I just feel like I’m barely holding it together.\n\nDoctor: Has your current episode of feeling sad, empty, and hopeless lasted for two years or more?\n\nPatient: No… not two years. It’s been about a year and two months, since after I graduated and started college classes and working full-time at the clinic.\n\nDoctor: Have you had an episode like this before?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Not like this, I don’t think… maybe I’ve had rough times before, but I don’t remember it being this bad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
